tiitiin qopursa ilanir 0 ‘Whoever stirs up smoke cannot help getting covered with soot.” The P

Eulogizing a man: “He was one who let his food be tasted, one who turned back his enemies, one
who twisted their necks; until death brought him down.” 0

i: UPLA’N QUTURDIY ovlan quturdi “The baby was high spirited and mischievous _qutur-
(irtaha, lajja fi mujiin).” :: TARIT QUTUR'DIY tariy quturdi “The seed (or plant, or other) grew
quickly (zaka).” Its root-meaning is that one says of anything that “goes beyond its measure
(jawaza ‘an miqdar)”: QUTURDIY quturdi. QUTURMA’Q quturmag. 0

bellowing for joy at it.” Also, they say of a bowl that is “filled to the brim (ywyatu ila asbariha)”"
with water so that it nearly overflows: KUSAR‘DIY ké8ardi.

opportunity and stole a colt which he owned with me in partnership (?);! he drove away my
sheep, and then departed (faraqa).” 0

He broke up our partnership (?)!
He grabbed a colt and left

[In explanation of the word ortuqluqin:] LIQIN: LIKIN: -ligin, -likin are two particles G
meaning “with (ma‘a).” The lam has either raf* (U) or kasr (1). In some contexts it corresponds
to the [Arabic prepositional] ba’ of attachment.? Example :: BLIKIN "ULUI*LUQA’ TAK-DIM
biligin uluylugqga tagdim ‘Through (bi-) wisdom I attained honor.” :: ’ADKUVLUKUN KAL:
‘TYSIZ‘LIKIN KAL-MA’ ddgiiliikiin kl ésizlikin kalma ‘Come with good (bi-xayr) do not come }
with evil (biSarr).” The root-form is with gaf, which is replaced by kaf in words that have kaf
313 ‘Thus the text; helping and vying should be reversed, or else minig birla should read mana. |
314 'Clauson (ED, 211) reads artuqluqin and translates ‘the stripped off the surplus (ie. profit).” The |
reading ortuqlugin seems to accord better with the translation; “which he owned with me in partnership” i
is in the text: a‘anni fi &sirka; a‘anni for a‘anna-ni could refer to the type of partnership known as ‘inan;
but note that the Arabic lexicons gloss ‘anna with sabba, which is the usual equivalent of sdk- (526), i.e. i
“to insult”; but sdkiiS- (322) can also be taken as from sék- (275) ‘“‘to tear out or tear down.” The gram-
matical explanation below only confounds the dilemma; the ‘‘particle” in question is only the instrumental |
suffix -in, which may be attached to the abstract noun suffix -liq/-lik, but also may not, as the case of biligin
illustrates. |

or imala, as | explained above. The lam has raf‘ (U) when the word it is affixed to has raf‘ in
the middle (ie. U in the ultima); it has kasr (I) when the word it is affixed to has kasr or is
unvowelled in the middle.

i: ULA’R: IKY TAWR: QUNUS:DIY olar ekki tawar qunusdi “Each of them stole qunué-
(salaba) the other’s property.” The same for vying or helping. QUNUS:!VR: QUNSMA’Q

i: YKIT LA’R ’TYSQA’ QINISDY yigitlar i8qa qanigdi “The young men were happy qanis-
(irtaha) in their work.” This means that they took pleasure (ihtazzii) in something. QINISUVR:
QINISMA’Q qanisir qanismag. 0

Rule. The Sin in this section comprises several meanings. The root of these verbs is G
biliteral. Thus :: "AR: TAWA’R: QAB'TIY @r tawar qapti ‘The man stole (salaba) the property.”

:: "UL "ANY TAB:DIY ol ani tapdi ‘He kicked (rakala) him (with his foot).’”? The insertion of
¥in indicates mutuality of the action (al-mufa‘ala), between two or more. Thus :: UL MANIK
BRLA’ TUBQ: QABUS:DIY ol manig birli topiq qapuSdi ‘He contended with me in snatching
(xalasani) the ball.” :: "UL MANIG BIR:LA’ TABIS:DIY ol minig birld tapi8di “He vied with me
in kicking (rakalani).’ 0 Then there are four categories that comprise the usage of this form.

1) It occurs with the meaning of giving help (al-i‘ana) in the action. Thus :: ’UL
MANK’ TUVN TIKISDY ol mana ton tiki8di ‘He helped me sew (a‘anani fi xiyata) the gar-
ment.” :: UL MANKA’ BITIK: BTIS:DIY ol mana bitig bitiSdi “He helped me write (kitba) the
book.”

2) It occurs with the meaning of vying (al-mubarat) in the action—to see which of the
two is the more skillful, the more courageous, the stronger, or the more resourceful in that
action, Thus :: "UL MANIK: BR-LA’ JALIS-DIY ol manig birla éali8di “He wrestled (sara‘a)
with me (to see which of us was the better or the stronger wrestler).” :: "UL MANIK: BIR:LA’

YA’ QURUS‘TIY ol minig birlé ya quruati “He contended with me in stringing (watara ma‘i)
the bow (to see which of us was the stronger in stringing).”

The distinction between helping and vying is as follows. When you mention the par-
ticle: MANKA’ mana meaning ‘‘to me (li)” then it has the meaning of helping.

{M. 91/114] 326
When you join to the verb the particle: MANIK: BIR-LA’ minig birlé meaning “with me (ma‘i)””
then it has the meaning of vying.

3) When lam and Sin are suffixed to a noun, that noun becomes the name of the stake
(xatar) in games of chance, racing, and shooting. Thus :: "UY-NA’DIM: ’AT-LASUV oynadim
atla8u “I played, with a horse as the stake (la‘ibtu wa-ja‘altu l-xatar fihi l-faras).” :: "UY: NA’-

DIM: ’AL:TUNLASUV oynadim altunla’u “I played with him and made gold the stake.” In this
there is a type of vying.

4) It occurs as a simple verb, without connoting any of the above meanings. Thus ::
KAMISDIY NA’NKIN kamidi nanni “He threw (taraha) the thing.” :: "UL ’ANIK BIR‘LA’
KARIS-DIY ol anig birlé karigdi “He quarreled (jadala) with him.” So understand!

The scope G
[Il 95/118] 328
of the qaf and kaf is of two types.
1) They form a verb which acts upon the subject (fil waqi‘ ‘ala man huwa maylub
fihi), Example :: BASIQTY ’AR: bassiqti dr ‘The man was attacked at night (buyyita)”—mean-
ing that the enemy attacked him; the root is: BAS-DIY basdi [268-9]. :: TUVN: KIRKTY
ton kirikti “‘The garment became soiled (yalaba d-daran ‘ala t-tawb).””
2) They form a class of verbal roots (asl li-L-bab). Example :: "AR: BALIQ:TY ar
baligti “The man was wounded (juriha)”; BALIQA’R: BALIQMA’Q baliqar baliqmaq. ::
JULUK-TY ’AR: Giiliikti_ dr “The man’s appearance was shabby.”

i: AM-KA’K-TIN TINIL‘DY admgiktin tinildi “Rest was had (usturiha) from toil and tinil-
trouble.” This is an intransitive verb whose agent is not named [ie. impersoml]. There are G
many examples of it in this language. In Arabic, not every intransitive verb may be made into a
verb whose agent is not named; but this is often done in Turkic. Example :: ’AWKA’ BARIL--

Rule. This section is based on lam. It has three aspects. G
: 1) It forms triliteral verbs that are compounded of biliteral roots plus the suffix lam
which is a marker of verbs whose agent is not named [i.e. passive]. Example :: YA’ QURUL‘DIY

===== part 2 page 40 =====
}
33
ya quruldi ‘The bow was strung (wuttira).”’ :: ’AR+ ?URULDY 4r uruldi “The man was struck
(duriba)”— this has a chapter of its own [105ff.]. The root is: QURDIY qurdi, ’UR-DIY urdi.
2) It forms impersonal (majhiil) verbs from intransitive ones. This departs from the
tule for Arabic. Example :: ?UL NA’NK KURL-DY ol nin kGriildi “That thing was seen (ru’iya).”
:: UL YIYR-K’ BARILDY ol yérka barildi “That place was gone to (duhiba).”
This usage sometimes agrees with that of the following letter [ie. n]. Example :: BTIK
BTIN-DIY bitig bitindi “‘The book was written (kutiba)”—BITIL-DIY bitildi means the same. ::
°AR- “ITIN-DIY 4t itindi “The man crawled (tazahhafa)”—’1TILDY itildi is a variant.
3) It forms independent verbs. Example :: "ULUK: TIRIL-DIY Gliig tirildi, SUVT-
SUIUL-DY sit suyuldi—meaning, “The dead came to life (hayiya),” and “The milk dried up in
the udder (baku’a).” :: SUVW SUZUL-DY siiw siiziildi “The water was purified (safa).”

Cigil, one says: "UL TAKIN-DIY ol tagindi meaning “He has arrived (hadara).” Its real meaning D
is “He has been pleased to arrive (taballaya bi-l-wusil).” The same for departing (dahaba) ::

This section has several aspects. G
1) It expresses that the agent does the action independently and without asking the i
aid of anyone else.’ The root of this type isa biliteral verb, Example :: "UL’AWKA’ BARIN-- i
DIY ol awka barindi “He pretended to go home but did not actually go.” :: UL ’AS TATINDY '
ol a8 tatindi “He pretended to taste the food but did not actually do so.” This type is like the :
Arabic pattern tafa‘ul, as in the phrase tasamma r-rajul “{the man] pretended to be deaf”; |
and ta‘agala ‘the pretended to be wise but was not.” |
2) It expresses that the agent does the action alone and in defiance of others. Exam- |
ple :: “AR: ’AL/IMIN:? ’ALIN-DIY r almin alindi “The creditor collected (qabd) his debt by |
himself.” :: ?UL TARIT TARIN-DIY ol tariy tarindi “The man tilled for himself, independently '
(istabadda . . . bi-z-zira‘a li-nafsihi).” When this is the meaning one may mention the self (nafs) |
by saying: "UVZINK’ ézinf meaning “for himself (li-nafsihi),” or: "UVZIY zi meaning “him- i"
self (nafsuhu).” The kaf (ie. the suffix -ka] is suffixed with the meaning of lam [i.e. the prefix |
li- in Arabic]. 4
When, however, the verb expresses that one pretends something without actually doing |
it, then one may not mention the self. |
|
The difference between the two [may be explained as follows]. Actions are common to
mankind, Now if one person from among the whole takes it upon himself [to perform an |
action], then it is mentioned that the agent has undertaken this action directly and without ask- |
ing the aid of anyone else. The description would be deficient if one did not mention the self
in that case; if one does mention the self along with it,
346 ‘This belongs with 2); the sentence here should read, ‘It expresses that the agent pretends to do
the action but does not actually do it.”
? First sukiin (-) crossed out.

===== part 2 page 50 =====
43
(Il. 126/159] 347
then the description becomes complete, and the action pertains to the agent alone, thus depart-
ing from the common category. This is like the saying of the Most High: fa-qtulii anfusakum
(Q.2:54/51 “and slay one another”). Since killing is common to mankind, these ones are singled
| out from among the whole and are commanded to perform the action directly upon themselves.
The description in this case would not be complete without the mention of the self, and so it is
| mentioned, But if it has the meaning of pretending, or if the verb has an intransitive sense, or
| if there is no sense in which it is common [to others], then the self, even though it is not men-
tioned with the verb, is as if it is mentioned, since an intransitive verb implies it. The description
| in that case is complete. This is like the saying of the Most High: kalla inna L-insana la-yatya
| an ra’ahu stayna (Q.96:6-7 “No indeed; surely Man waxes insolent,/ for he thinks himself self-
sufficient”). The Exalted One does not say: an ra’a nafsahu stayna. This is like our saying in
Turkic: ’IY5-QA’ BAQIN i8qa bagin meaning “Look at the end of your deed (unzur axir am-
rika)”; :: QURIN qurin meaning “Dry yourself (tajaffaf)'”; :: SUWQA’ KIRIN suwga kirin
| meaning “Wash yourself (iytasil).””
3) It agrees? with the section with lam, the niin being an alternant of it. Example ::
BITIK BITIL-DIY BITIN-DIY bitig bitildi, bitindi “The book was written (kutiba).” Both [are
correct]. This is a marker of the passive verb. In Arabic [also] lam and niin alternate, as in the
phrases: rajul satn al-asabi‘ wa-8atluha® (“a man with rough fingers’), and kabn ad-dalw wa-
kabluhu* (‘the fold of a leathern bucket’’)—it is the part of its rim which bends.
4) It occurs as a simple verb without expressing any of the above meanings. Example
:: TANKRIYK’ TABIN-DY tanyika tapindi “He worshipped (‘abada) God Most High.” :: TANK-
RIYKA SIDIN-DIM tanyikai siyindim “I took refuge (‘udtu) with God Most High.”
These are the various meanings as well for the chapters of weak words, nasal words,
etc., both simple and compound.

1) It transitivizes a verb. Example ::’AR- SUVW TAMUZ-DIY ar suw tamuzdi ‘The

2) It forms simple verbs. Example :: TABUZ-TUQ. TABIZ-DIY tapuzyuq tapizdi “He

The Sin-section has been explained above [325-326].

The qaf- and kaf-section is of two types [cf. 327-328 G}:

1) When kaf or qaf is suffixed to a biliteral verb, the meaning is that the object of the
action is overcome by it unwillingly.’ Example :: ’AR- YATTYTA’ [sic], BASIQ-TY ar yayiga
basiqti “The man was attacked at night and captured by the enemy.” :: ’AR- USUQTIY ar
usuqti “The man was overcome with thirst.” Their roots are: BUS-DIY ’US-DIY basdi, usdi.
Example of kaf :: TVN KIURIK-TIY ton kirikti “The garment was overcome with dirt.” ::
*AR- KWNIK-TIY dr kenikti “The man was overcome with weakness so that he tarried behind
his companions.”

2) It forms simple verbs. Example :: “AR: “AWK’ "ASUQ-TY ar awkd aSuqti “The
man longed for home.” 0 ::’AR-’TYSIY JULUK:-TIY 4r i8i Gilliikti “The man’s condition was
shabby.”

The lam and niin have been discussed above [ 337, 346-347].

The infinitive of this [chapter] is according to the pattern fa‘allal.? Example ::
QAJURMA’Q qaéurmaq—the second radical is vowelled.

{I. 130/166] 350
YUK KUTURMA’K yiik kotiirmak “Lifting the load.” This is the sound infinitive.

The infinitive construct, on the other hand, is according to the pattern fu‘luli, with
unvowelled second radical. Example :: ’”ANIK YUK KUT-RUKIY! KUR. anig yiik kotriigi kor
“Look at his lifting the load.” :: NIK SUVW- KAJURKIY KUR: anig siw kaériigi kér “Look
at his crossing the water.” I have explained to you above the place of yayn or kaf in this form

[286].

“triliteral.” Example :: TANKRYK’ TABIN tanyika tapin “Worship God Most High.” :; TANK-
RIYKA’ YUKUN tanyika yiikiin “Bow down to God Most High.” :: SARIN sarin “Be patient.”

The active participle of this chapter is: TABIN-TUJY tapinyudi “Worshipper,” YUKUN-

The participle which expresses duration of the action: TABIN-TA’N YUKUNKA’N.
tapinyan, yiikiingin. The determination of qaf, kaf, yayn, igba‘, etc. in this form is as I have

The participle which expresses that one is desirous of performing the action :: TABUI--
SA’Q: AR; tapuysaq r “A man who desires to serve.” |
[Il 132/168] 351
This type of adjectival occurs only rarely with triliteral and multiliteral verbs. The rule holds for
all. All that exceed the biliteral drop the last part of the stem before being joined to the suffix
which carries the meaning of this adjectival. Example :: TABIN-DIY tapindi “He worshipped”;
the adjectival is :: TABUT'SA’Q tapuysaq—the niin drops and the word reverts to its root, namely
the word: TABUT tapuy “Service.”
The participle which expresses that one ought to, or is resolved to, perform the action
is on the pattern fa‘ul‘ulul, the two ‘ayn’s (second and fourth radicals) are vowelled with one of
the three vowels, and the two lam’s (third and sixth radicals) are unvowelled. Example :: BAK-
YA’ZUQ KAJURK-LUK ’AR-DIY beg yazuq kdtiirgiiliik ardi “The emir should have forgiven the
sin”; :: "UL TANKRIYKA’ TABIN-TULUQ. ’AR-DIY ol tanyiki tapinyuluq ardi “He should
have worshipped God Most High.” The rule is to add this suffix to the stem, taking into account
those words with or without qaf, etc. This type contains something of the meaning of the infini-
tive.

i: TANKRIY ’UPUL- TUP-TURDIY  tanzi oyul tuyturdi “God brought forth (awlada) _ twytur- |
a child (from the woman).” Its root is) TUTUR-TURDIY [sic] tuyurdi—according to the rule G |
that any verb that is transitive without the suffix ta’ [ie. in this case twyur- “to give birth”] is

the water to be drunk (airaba)”; if you say: SUVW: ’IJ-TUR-DIY siw iétiirdi the meaning is:

pick them”); naqata L-mushaf (‘He pointed the text”) and angatahu ‘yayrahu (‘He had someone
else point it”)—with the addition of the alif the verb becomes doubly transitive. The same in

Describing a man who deceived him, he advises a person: “If he comes to you travelling on his
way, then wake! him from his sleep (and inform him of his deed, so that) he will go away
worried (muhtamman), (since) he sold my servant [lit. my moon] (without my permission).”

This section has four functions: G

(1) To transitivize a biliteral. Example :: BAR-DIY bardi “He went (dahaba).” :: BAR-
TUR-DIY barturdi “Someone else made him go (adhabahu ‘yayruhu).” +: AR: SUWQA’ QAR~
DIY ar suwga qardi “The man choked (Sariqa) on the water”; :: "ANIY SUW:QA’ QAR: TUR~
DIY ani suwga garturdi “He made him choke on it.” These are cases of intransitive biliteral
verbs being made transitive in this chapter.

Or else you may begin with biliteral verbs that are transitive and suffix ta’ and ra’ [to

make them doubly transitive]. Example :: AR: YA’ QUR-DIY 4r ya qurdi “The man strung
(wattara) the bow’’—here there is one agent; but if you wish to say that someone else brought
him to do it, or put upon him the task of performing the action, you add the suffix
| [Il 157/198] 366
| ta’ and ra’ :: °UL’ANKAR YA’ QUR:TUR:DIY ol anar ya qurturdi ‘‘He assigned him the task of
stringing the bow and he strung it (kallafahu ‘ala tawtir al-qaws fa-wattara)”—here there are two
| agents, one of them ordering, the other performing. This is like [the Arabic] phrases: naqata
r-rajul al-mushaf (“The man pointed the manuscript”) and angatahu ‘yayruhu (“Someone else
| made him point it”); lagata s-sunbul (‘He picked the ears of grain’) and alqafahu yayruhu
(“Someone had him pick them’’)—here too there are two agents.
| Or else you add yayn and ra’, or kaf and ra’. Example :: ’AT- TUR-DIY at turdi “The
horse (or other) was emaciated (huzila)”; :: AR- ATIN- TUR-TUR-DIY r atin turyurdi “The
| man emaciated (hazzala) his horse.” :: "AR: YUQA’RUV TURDY &r yogaru turdi “The man

===== part 2 page 69 =====
62
stood up (qama)”; :: "ANY TUR-TURDY ani turyurdi “Someone made him stand (aqama).”
:: YAT ’ARUVDIY yayiriidi “The butter melted (daba)”; :: ‘AR: YAT.’AR-KUR-DIY fir yay
argiirdi “The man melted (adaba) the butter.” :: °UQ- KUNDIY oq k6ndi “The arrow was :
straight (istaqama)”; :: "UQJY KUN:D-KAR-DIY! ogéi kOndgardi “The arrow-maker straight- i
ened (qawwama) the arrow.”
'
The ta’ and yayn and kaf are not what cause these verbs to be transitive, but rather it
is the ra’ alone that does this. The following is evidence for this. :: QUS: UJUR-DIY qu’ ;
uéurdi ‘‘He made the bird fly (atara)”; SUVW- KAJURDIY siw kiéiirdi “He took [him] across |
(a‘bara) the water.” In these verbs, transitivization is accomplished by the ra’ alone; the same is
the case here.
The reason for the insertion of ta’ or ‘Yayn or kaf is ease of pronunciation. If they were
not inserted, then in these verbs there would be joined together, in the trasitive form, two ra’s in |
the preterite and three ra’s in the aorist. Example :: QUT-TAR-DIY qutyardi “He saved him”— |
if not for the Yayn you would have to say: QUT-RAR-DIY *qutrardi. :: SUWITAR-DIY suw- I
ardi—you would have to say: SUWRAR-DIY *suwrardi. This is in the preterite. In the aorist |
you would have to say: QUT-RAR-DPI¥? *qutrarur to mean “He will save him” and: SUW- |
RARDY¥? *suwrarur to mean “He will give water.”’ One of these ra’s would belong to the root,® |
the second would be the ra’ of transitivization, and the third would be the ra’ of the aorist. |
Therefore the ra’ of the root changes to fa’ in hard (sulba) stems, to yayn in stems with gaf or I
igba‘, and to kaf in stems with kaf or rikka.
[IL 158/200] 367 |
f
The purpose is euphony.! |
(2) To transitivize a triliteral verb that is intransitive. Example :: ’AR: TIRL-DIY |
ar tirildi “The man came to life (hayiya)”; :: TANK-RIY TIRKUR-DIY tanyi tirgiirdi “God
brought him to life (ahya).” :: "AR+ QZIL-DIY 4r qizildi “The man was punished (inzajara...
lamma daqa wabal amrihi)”; ANY QUZ-TUR-DIY ani qizyurdi “Someone punished him.” |
(3) To form an intransitive verb meaning that one almost did something, or intended
actually to do it but came to it very gradually, Example :: SUVW- TAMJIR-DIY saw taméirdi
“The water came down in a light shower (taraSa3a... . wa-sariba qalilan) from the cloud.” ::
Sly TAS-TUR-DIY edié taSyurdi “The kettle nearly overflowed (kadat ... an tafiira).”’ When
the verb has this meaning, the second? consonant is unvowelled? in the preterite (madi) and in
the aorist (yabir), but the fourth consonant has fatha (A) in the aorist.

To express, in this chapter, that someone else helped one, or vied with one, in the
action, you add Sin before the dal. Example :: "UL MANK’ ’AT- SUWTARIS-DIY ol mana at
suwyariSdi “He helped me water the horse.” :: "UL MANIK BIRLA’ QARIN- TUD-TURUS-DIY
ol manig birla qarin todyuruddi “He vied with me in satiating the belly.”

To express that one pretended to do the action but did not actually do it, there are two
ways:

(1) To add niin before the dal, as explained above. Example :: UL ’AT- SUWIAR--

DIY‘ ol at suwyarindi “He pretended to water (uriya annahu yusqi) the horse.” :: "UL QARIN
TUD-TURUN-DIY ol garin todyurundi ‘“{He pretended] to satiate the belly, or else he acted
independently in this action.”

(2) The more correct, and better way,

{II 160/202] 368
and the one more used, is to add mim and sin! before the niin, making [a suffix of] three con-

sonants counting the nan. Example :: UL ’AT- SUWPARIM-SIN-DIY ol at suwyarimsindi

“He pretended to water the horse.” The mim and nin are unvowelled and the sin is vowelled

with kasr (I). The explanation of this will appear in its proper place [396-398].

This section has four aspects: G i
(1) With the meaning of helping or vying in the action. Example :: ?UL MANK’ YUK |
KUTURUSDIY ol mana yiik kétriiSdi “He helped me carry (haml) the load.” I have already |
mentioned [325-326] that the particle: MANK’ mana implies helping, and the particle: MANIK
BiR-LA’ minig birla implies vying. Example :: "UL MANIK BIRLA’ SAK-RIS-DY ol mianig birli
sekri8di ‘He vied with me in jumping, to see which of us was stronger in it (watabani mubaratan |
li-yura ayyuna ajlad fihi).”
(2) Asa branch-off? from biliterals. Example :: QAJ-TIY qaéti “He fled,” QAJURDIY f
qaéurdi “He caused to flee,” QAJRUS:DIY qaéruadi “The fled from each other.” The ¥in
implies reciprocity. :: SUVW KAJ-TIY stw k&@ti “He crossed the water,” KAJUR-DIY katiirdi
“He caused him to cross,” KAJ-RUS:DIY kaériisdi “They caused each other to cross, or helped
each other across.” A letter is added to the biliteral and it becomes triliteral; a letter is added to
the triliteral and it becomes quadriliteral.
(3) As a verb formed from biliteral? nouns and taking on the meaning of a stake i
(xifar, qimar). The suffix is lam and Sin, Example :: "UL MANIK BRLA’’UQ-: ’ATIY ’TLASUV |

| This section has four aspects. G
| (1) To mean that one came into possession (sara sahib) of the thing named. Example
| :: "URA’TUT BAK-LAN-DIY urayut beglindi “The woman took on a husband.” :: ’AR- ANIY
QIZ-LAN-DIY 4r ani qizlandi ‘“The man adopted her as a daughter.”
:
| (2) To form a verb from a biliteral noun. Example :: ’AR- ’AT-LAN-DIY ar atlandi
| “The man mounted the horse.” 0 :: ’AT: TAR-LAN-DIY at tarlandi ‘“‘The horse (or other)
sweated.”
| (3) To mean that the agent undertook the action by himself (tawalla iqama dalika Lfi‘l
| bi-nafsihi), Example :: *AR- ’UVZINKA’ YMIS ’AW-DIN-DIY ar 6zing yemi8 awdindi “The man
picked the fruit by himself” :: UL ’VZINKA’ ’AT: SUKUL-NDY ol Szind at ségliindi “He
| roasted the meat alone and did not ask the help of anyone else.”
| Nouns and verbs ending in a vowel (min dawat al-arba‘a) may become sound (qad
yajri . . . majra s-sahi) in this section because the weak letter is dropped with the tightening
(salaba) of the pronunciation. Example :: ’AR- QAR-TIN- QARTAN-DIY Gr gartin qartandi
“The man treated his own wound’’—this is like an intransitive verb; its root is: QARTA’DIY
qartadi. :; “AR- KUK-LANDIY &r kiiglandi “The man sang a song’”’—its root is: KUVK-LA’DIY
kiglidi = :: QUVR QURSAN-DIY qiir qur’andi “He put on a belt”—its root is:s QUVR-SA’DIY
girSadi.
| 392 3 Altered to yaniya.

===== part 2 page 97 =====
| |
90 :
+
(4) To form a simple verb without any of these meanings. Example :: TAWAY KAW-- :
SAN-DIY tewe kaw8andi “The camel ruminated.” ’AR- TURQUN-DIY 4r turgindi “The man
was ashamed to undertake a matter.” JIT-RIY TAZKIN-DIY éiyri tazgindi ‘The pulley (or
other) turned.” |
[U. 202/255] 394
Rule. The imperative of this chapter has four consonants. Example :: MANIY TUD--
TUR: mani todyur “‘Satiate me.” :: ’AT- SUW-TAR. at suwyar “Water the horse.” :: MANIY
°UD-TUR mini odyur “Wake me.”
If in the ra’ section you wish to make the verb doubly transitive you add ta’ after the
ra’ and this assimilates to the dai, resulting in a doubled ta’, Example :: "UL ’ATIN SUWPAR-TY
ol atin suwyartti “He had someone water his horse’’—there is here a joining of two unvowelled
consonants (jam‘ bayn as-sakinayn). :: MAN ’ANY TUD-TURTUM min ani todyurttum “I
ordered that he be satiated.” Understand!
The active participle is: SUWPAR-TUVJIY TUD-TURI'VJY suwyaryudi, todyuryadi
for the Turks; TUD-TUR-DAJY SUWI'RDAJY todyurdaéi, suwyardagi for the Oyuz. D
The active participle expressing duration of the action: "UDTURI‘A’N. TUD-TUR-
TA’N odyuryan, todyuryan in words with gaf or yayn or iba‘; or, in words with kaf: KUL-
SIRKA’N ’R- kiilsirgiin dr “A man who always smiles (mubsam).” 0 Or, of a camel :: KAW-
SANKA’N- TAWAY kawSangan tewe “A camel that ruminates alot.” 0
The active participle expressing that one ought to or intends to perform the action.
Example :: "UL ’ANIY TUD-TURTULUQ ’AR-DIY ol ani todyuryuluq 4rdi “He should have
satiated him.”
The active participle expressing that one intends or desires to perform the action.
Example :: ’L AT: SUW-T'ARID:SA’Q ’AR-DIY ol at suwyariysaq ardi ‘He intended, or desired,
to water the horse.” :: UL ’ANY SUW-DIN: KJRUS-SA’K ?AR-DIY ol ani suwdin kiériiisak
ardi “It was his intention and desire to help him cross the water.” However this type is rare.
The active participle expressing that one has in mind
{IL 204/257] 395
to perform an action, or else an action that one has transferred (? fi‘l qad amdahu). Example ::
"UL “AT SUWDPARIP:LIY ’AR-DIY ol at suwyariyli ardi “He was watering (kana sdqiy®™) the
horse.” :: UL ’ANIY TUD-TURI-LY ’UL ol ani todyuruyli ol “He is feeding (muSabbi‘) him.”
The last letter of the stem is vocalized in this case.
Passive participle: ’UD-TUR-MIS. "UL [sic] odyurmis # “A man who has been
wakened (miiqaz).”” TUD-TUR-MIS todyurmié “One who is satiated (musabba‘).”” It is formed
in a single way, as has been shown above [297].

any noun you wish, whether triliteral, quadriliteral, or other.

The root of these verbs is biliteral and to it is added mim sin niin thus making it quin- G
quiliteral, These letters may also be added to triliterals, quadriliterals and quinquiliterals with
the same meaning. It is equivalent to the Arabic pattern tafa‘ul [in such phrases as] tayafala
r-rajul meaning “[The man] pretended to be negligent about it although he was not negligent,”
and tadahaka meaning ‘He pretended to laugh but was not really laughing.” Thus.
When this is the meaning desired, the rule is that the last letter of the root word is
vocalized with fath (A) if [the root word] has fath, damm (U) if it has damm, and kasr (I) if it
395 "MS. mujazat.

has kasr, Example. Fath :: "UL ’ANDIN NA’NK TILAM-SIN-DIY ol andin nan, tilamsindi “He |
pretended to ask for something from him.” :: UL BIJA’K- BILAM-SIN-DIY ol biéak bilimsindi
“He pretended to be sharpening the knife but was not really doing it.” The imperative of these
is: TIYLA’ tilé “Seek!” BIYLA’ bila “Sharpen!” The lam has fath in the imperative, and
remains thus. 0 Damm :: UL MANK’ TALIM: KULUM-SINDY ol mana tilim kiiliimsindi
“He pretended to laugh a lot.” :: "UL YUQA’RUV TURM-SINDIY ol yoqaru turumsindi “He |
pretended to be standing up but was not really standing.” The imperative of these is: KUL: |
kill “Laugh!” TUR: tur “Stand!” The lam and ra’ are unvowelled in the imperative; they take
damm (U) when this suffix is added, following the vowel of the preceding consonant which is |
the first consonant of the word [i.e. in vocalic harmony with the first syllable]. © Butin the
case where the biliteral root has fath (A) then it takes kasr (I) and does not follow the fatha
[of the preceding consonant—i.e. there is no vocalic harmony]. Example :: UL "AWKA’
BARIM-SINDY ol awk barimsindi ‘He pretended to go home.” :: ’AR+ SUW: QA’ QARIM--
SINDY dr suwga garimsindi “[The man] pretended to choke on the water.”
[Il. 207/260] 397
The final consonant does not take fatha in these verbs, as I have explained. When the first letter
of a biliteral has kasr (I) it remains thus. Example :: UL ’AWK’ KIRIM-SIN-DIY ol dwka
kirimsindi ‘‘He pretended to enter the house.” :: "UL YAR-MA’Q: TIRM-SIN-DY ol yarmaq
terimsindi “‘He pretended to collect the dirhams (or other).”
The mim is always unvowelled. The sin always takes kasr (1). The niin is also unvow-
elled. Only the vowel before the first consonant of the suffix changes.
What I have discussed to this point pertains to biliterals. 0
Triliterals. :: "UL ’ANY QAJRM-SINDIY ol ani gaérumsindi “He pretended to drive
him away’ but did not really do so.” :: °UL ’ANIK-: YA’ZUQIN- KAJRUM‘SIN-DIY ol anig
yazuqin kdériimsindi “He pretended to forgive his sin.” The ra’ in these examples has damm
(U) since in the word: QAJUR: qaéur the jim has damm. The jim becomes unvowelled in this
section, the damma (U) going over to the ra’. In triliterals the vowel is taken from the middle
since it is closer to the last letter of the word.
Quadriliterals. :: "AR: ’AT- SUW-T'ARIM-SIN-DIY dr at suwyarimsindi “The man
pretended to water the horse.” The ra’ here takes kasr (I) even though the yayn” has fatha (A)
since fatha does not stand opposed to kasra; just as in the biliterals you say: BARIM:SIN-DY
barimsindi meaning ‘‘He pretended to go.’ But in the word: TILAM-SIN-DIY tilimsindi mean-
ing “He pretended to seek,” you do not say TILAM-SIN-DIY [sic] tilimsindi since it belongs to
the roots ending in a vowel (dawat al-arba‘a), its root being: TILA’ tili—the alif is dropped in |
order that the suffix may combine with the stem; thus the alif disappears in pronunciation and
is dropped in writing. After the alif is dropped it is not permitted to have kasr (I) after the lam
lest the triliteral look like the biliteral, namely ::

This section has several aspects: G
(1) To mean that one has become owner or master [of the thing]. Example :: "AR
TARII-LAN-DIY dr tariylandi ‘‘The man became owner of a field (hart).” +: AR» QASUQ.-
LANDY &r qaguqlandi “The man became owner of a spoon.”
(2) To mean that one considers oneself to belong to the category of what is mentioned,
or dresses in their dress. Example :: ’AR- ’UTUZ:LAN-DIY 4r oyuzlandi “The man dressed like
the Oyuz and considered himself one of them.” ::’AR- JIKILAN-DIY 4r éigillindi “The man
dressed like the Cigil.” This is like the Arabic [verse]: wa-qaysa ‘aylana wa-man taqayyasa,
meaning “[Qays ‘Aylin and those who] dress in their manner.” This is a general rule holding for
all verbs. -
(3) To mean that the thing naturally brought it forth. Example :: YA’J BUTAIQ- |
LANDY yiyaé butaqlandi/butiqlandi “The tree brought forth branches (axraja . . . al-aysan).”

(4) As a simple verb without any of these meanings. Example :: ’AR- QATIT-LANDY
ar qatiylandi ‘The man exerted himself (ijtahada).”” KISIY YUMURLAN:DY kisi yumurlandi

The root of all of these verbs is a triliteral noun from which the verb is formed. By
analogy with these you may form verbs from quadriliterals, quinquiliterals, or what exceeds that.
Therefore know it well, that you gain in wisdom.

a
102 |
The imperative of this chapter is made up of six consonants. Example :: SUK-MAN- G / |
LAN: sékminlin “Dress like a warrior.”? :: KIR-SAN-LAN- kirSanlan “Coat your face with |
white lead.” 0
This section has
[Il 222/278] 406
four aspects:
(1) To mean that one considers oneself to belong to the class of the thing named, or
dresses in that manner. Example :: ’AR- QIF-JA’Q:LAN-DIY dr qiféaqlandi, ’AR- ’Ul-RAQ-
LAN-DY 4r oyraqlandi “He dressed in the manner of Qiféaq or Oyraq.”
(2) To mean that one has become master or possessor of the thing named. Example
i: ’AR QIS-RAQ-LAN-DIY 4r qisraqlandi “The man became owner of a mare.” :; ’AR YAR-
MAQLANDIY &r yarmaqlandi ‘‘The man got a dirham.”
(3) To be a verb formed from a quadriliteral noun. Example :: *ANIK KUVZIY
JAL-BAK:LAN-DY anig kézi ¢élpaklindi “His eye was bleary.” :: NIK KVZY KIRBUKLAN--
DIY anig kézi kirpiklndi “The lash (hadab naxis) in his eye grew.”
(4) To be a simple verb, Example :: TAR: BURJAQ-LAN-DY tar buréaqlandi “The
sweat formed beads.” :: YILA’N JUQMQLAN-DIY? yilan éaqmaqlandi “The snake coiled.” 0
All sextiliteral verbs of this type are formed from quadriliteral nouns; there is no inde-
pendent verb. If you wish to form verbs in this meaning from any biliteral, triliteral or quadri-
literal noun, the way to do it is to add the suffix lam nan, thus forming a verb that is conjugated
in these ways. [This is] a general rule holding good without exception for all the dialects (Iuyat),
and therefore it should be memorized.
End of the Book of Sound Words

BUQUQ boquq “Goiter (lahm yudadi) occurring between the skin and the flesh
[I 228/285] 409
on both sides of the Adam’s-apple.” In Faryana and the regions of Siqni there are groups of
people who are characterized by this defect. Each generation acquires these swellings (yatahaw-
saliina bihi) anew. Sometimes they are so large that they prevent people from seeing their own
chests and feet. I asked them about the origin of this, and they told me the following story:
“Our forebears were loud-mouthed infidels. The companions of the Prophet of God
(may God bless and keep him) made a raid against them. Our forebears attacked them at night
L with loud screams and yells, and the Muslims were put to flight at the sound. The news reached
‘Umar (may God be pleased with him). He put a curse upon them, and this defect appeared on
their throats.”
It has remained among them asa legacy. Now there is never seen among them one who
isloud-mouthed. 0

QAR-PA’Q [sic] qaryay “Cursing (la‘n).” Thus :: TANK-RIY QAR-TATINKA’ — garyay

QIARTAT: qiryay “The anger (yadab) of an emir or king at whoever is below him.”
Hence :: XA’N- ’NY QIR-T'A’DIY xan ani qiryadi “The king was angry with him and shunned
him.”
They distinguish between the cursing of a slave [i.e., man] by God, the Most Great and
Powerful, and the cursing by one of His slaves of one of his subjects who is like him. The former
has fatha [i.e. qaryay], the latter kasra [i.e. qiryay]. This is similar to the distinction they make
between the Messenger of God Most High and the messenger of the king. They call the Messen-
ger of God Most High: YL’FAJ- yalawaé, and the messenger of the king: YALA’FAR.- yalawar, D
in Uighur dialect.

In the Name of God the Merciful the Compassionate
Book of Doubled Verbs
Chapter: Biliterals
Know that genuine doubling is rarely found. Verbs are only doubled by the combina- G
tion of a ta’ which is part of the word itself with a dal which is the preterite marker; then a
tadid appears and we call it “doubled.’”? Note that doubling does not occur in the aorist or the
infinitive, since in both of these the dal disappears. As for true doubling, it is rare, and we have
set it off in this section because of its infrequent occurrence. Thus ::
T

daraban).” TUTA’R: TUTM’Q tutar tutmaq. 0 (tit- ?)

Rule. The imperative in this chapter has two consonants, and therefore we have called G
it biliteral. Example :: ’AT- TUT: at tut “Take the horse.” :: TAWA’R: SAT: tawar sat “Sell
the merchandise.”
This type of verb is called [‘doubled”] only because of the joining of two consonants "
of the same genus in the preterite. This does not follow the course of Arabic [doubled roots] in
the aorist and infinitive, as you can see. However, since it is similar [to Arabic] in the preterite,
it is given that name.*

it is a pointed dal. I have heard this in Yayma, Tuxsi, Oyuz, and the extremities (atraf ?) of G
Uighur. The alternation of dal and dal is also permitted in Arabic, as in: ma duqtu ‘adufan?
wa-la ‘adufan (“I have not tasted a thing’), with either dal or dal.

- Rule. Doubling in Turkic only occurs with words ending in ta’ [i.e. “secondary” G
x doubling in verbal roots—see 411 and 412 G]. This doubling does not extend
[I 256/317] 424
E to all forms of the verbal conjugation. And it is rarely found in nouns. We call this type of verb
L “doubled” because of the junction of two consonants of the same genus in the preterite. One of
: these is a radical—part of the root’ of the word—and this is ta’. The other is dal which is the
; preterite marker; this becomes ta’ because of its proximity to the hard ta’ [ie., by assimilation],
: and thus doubling is produced.
: Doubling in the root is rarely found, as I wrote above [411]. 0 The imperative in
this chapter has three consonants. Example :: "AW: BAZAT- dw bizat “Paint the house”; :: :
BTIK BITIT- bitig bitit “Have the book written.””?
: The sound active participle in this chapter: BITIK BITIYKUVJIY bitig bititgiiéi “One
who has something written”; :: "AT- KUZT:KUVJIY at kézitgiéi “One who guards a horse.”
: In Oyuz and Qiféaq dialect: BITT-DAJY bititda¢i, KUZATJIY kéz’ttdéi—the root-form of D
: this is: KZT-DAJTY k6zatdaéi, assimilated.
: The active participle expressing duration of the action: one adds the suffix yayn alif
niin, or kaf in place of yayn according to the rule. Thus :: "UL ’ATIE KUZT-K’N ’ULol atiy
: kézdtgan ol “He always guards® the horse”; :: "UL ’AWIN BAZAT-KA’N ’UL ol dwin biazitgin ol
; “He always paints his house.” And in words with isba‘: "UL TARIT TARIT-TA’N ’UL ol tariy
: tarityan ol ‘‘He always orders tilling’; :: "UL BUPDAY- ’ARIT-T'A’N UL ol buyday arityan ol
“He always cleans the wheat (or other).””
E Words with kaf or yayn, and rikka or isba‘, follow the example of the first method (?
: yu‘tabaru .. , ‘ala L-minhaj al-awwal).
| The active participle expressing that one desires to perform the action. ::’UL TARID
s TARITYT'S’Q ’L ol tariy taritiysaq ol ‘He is eager and desirous to sow”; :: "UL *AWIN: BAZT-IK-
: SA’K: ’UL ol dwin bizatigsak ol “He is eager
[II. 257/319] 425
| and desirous to paint the house.” One adds yayn or kaf to the stem, plus sin, plus qaf for words
é with iba‘ or gaf or yayn; 0 but kaf is found at the beginning and the end [of the suffix] in
Q words with kf or imala or rikka, as was the rule for sound verbs [294-295].

The active participle expressing that one ought to perform the action or intends to do
so. :: UL TART TARIT-TILQ ’L ol tariy tarityulug ol “He intends to order the sowing”; :: "L |
°*AT- KUZTKILIK ’L ol at kézitgiiliik ol ‘He ought, or intends, to guard the horse.” The place 5
of yayn or kaf follows these examples (? yu‘tabaru mahall al-yayn wa-L-kaf fi mahalliha). Some 4
of the Oyuz make this lam a sin, as I have explained [296]. D t
The active participle expressing that one is about to perform the action which he has
in mind to do, :: UL TARIT TARITIL-LIY ’AR-DIY ol tariy taritiyli ardi “He had in mind a
to order the sowing, and was about to do it.” :: ’UL ’ATII KUZTIK-LIY ’AR-DIY ol atiy -
kOzatigli ardi “He intended and was on the point of guarding the horse.” This aspect approxi- 7

The difference between the nouns of time, place, and instrument is that, when the noun

L Know that this chapter has several aspects. One is to be a triliteral verb derived from G
L the deficient or final-weak chapter, intransitive, to which a ta’ has been added, thus transitivizing
the verb. The weak letter drops and ta’ takes its place, so the verb becomes like [i.e., again] a
L triliteral.
2 Example, with a defective verb! ::; AR: BUR:NIY QA’NADIY r burni qanadi “The
L man had a nosebleed”; it becomes transitive thus: "AR: BURNIN QANATIY dr burnin ganatti
' “He made the man’s nose bleed”; and in the imperative: "NINK BURNIN: QANAT- anin, burnin
' qanat “Make his nose bleed.” The alif has dropped from: QA’NADIY qanadi. :: UL BILIK-NY
| QUVQITIY ol bilikni q6qitti “He extinguished the lamp and made its fumes spread”; its root is:
| QUVQ-DIY qoqdi; and in the imperative it becomes: QUQIT: goqit—the vav has dropped. 0
|

Example, with a final-weak verb :: "AR- QUMIN-DY [sic] ar qomidi ‘The man was
aroused in a matter’’; this is an intransitive verb, and it becomes transitive thus: "ANY QUMIT-
I ani gomit “Arouse him in

[I 261/324] 427

i a matter’—the ya’ drops and ta’ takes its place. :: "ULIYDIY NA’NK Glidi nan “The thing
became wet”; it is transitivized thus: ’ULITY 6litti “Something made it wet”; and in the impera-
tive: ’ULIT- 6lit “Make it wet.”

Thus a defective or final-weak intransitive verb is made transitive by ta’. There is no use

: of the suffixes ra’ and yayn or kaf by which intransitive sound verbs are made transitive, as in:

: QUS ’UJ-DIY qué uédi “The bird flew”; transitive: "UJUR:DIY uéurdi “Something made it

: fly”; :: "ULK- TIRL-DIY lig tirildi “The dead came to life”; then: TANKRY TIR:KURDIY

tari tirgiirdi “God Most High brought him to life”; :: "AR TUD-TY r todti “The man was
satiated”; then: "UL TUD-TURDIY ol todyurdi ‘“‘He satiated him.” This is the rule in all chap-
ters of doubling. 0

: When the verb has a single agent [i.e. is singly transitive] in the deficient or final-weak
[ category, and ta’ is added, then it becomes transitive toward an object in regard to two agents,

: one of them ordering and the other carrying out the action [i.e. doubly transitive]. Example ::

: ?AR: BTIK BTIYDIY 4r bitig bitidi “The man wrote the book.” This is an action which arises

. from a single agent and is directed toward an object. Then: "UL ’ANKAR BTIK BITITIY ol

2 anar bitig bititti “He had him write the book.” The verb has become transitive toward an object

2 in regard to two agents. :: ’AR BJA’K: BILA’DIY 4r bié&k biladi “The man sharpened the

5 knife.” Then: BILATIY bilatti “He caused someone else to sharpen it.” The same.

[ The other aspect is to be a basic root belonging to the chapter and having nothing to do
, with the former meaning. Example :: TA’M: ’AMITIY tam 4mitti “The wall (or other) was

i Know that: ’DTIY -Adtiis a suffix that is attached to nouns forming verbs that indicate G
: natural qualities. Example: the word for “beauty” is: KUR:K kérk; to say that something was

: beautiful’ you attach the suffix: ’AD-TIY -ddti thus :: KURKA’D-TIY kérkidti meaning “It

a was beautiful.” The word for ‘‘ntelligence” is: BLIK bilig; then :: "UTLA’N- BLKA’D-TIY

a oylan bilgidti “The boy was intelligent.” Then the alif disappears” in pronunciation [i.e. the

7 vowel is shortened], and the dal assimilates to the ta’ giving rise to doubling.

Rule. The imperative in this chapter has four consonants. Example :: TAW-RAT- G i
tawrat “Hurry (‘ajjil)”; TAB- RAT. taprat “Move it (harrik).”’ Negative imperative: TAWRAT-M’ E
tawratma ‘Don’t hurry (1d ta‘jal)”; TABRAT-M’ tapratm’ ‘Don’t move it.” ' :
Root doubling in this chapter is that in which two consonants of the same genus occur | |
in the imperative. Example :: ‘IYSIT ’AR-TAT: i8iy artat “Corrupt (afsid)* the matter”; :: dg
?ANY SIPTAT. ani siytat “Make him cry.” |
|
The sound active participle in this chapter: TAWRATI'UJY tawratyuti “One who |
hurries (musri‘)” > TAB-RAT:KUJY tapratgii#i “One who moves something.” In the Oyuz dia- |
lects: TAB-RATAJIY TAWRATAJY taprittadi, tawrattadi. D :
The participle expressing continuity of the action: TAWRAT-I'A’N: tawratyan “One |
who is always going in a hurry (‘ajiil fi s-sayr)”; TABRAT:K’N tapratgin |
[IL 290/360] 443 | :
“One who moves things a great deal.” |

146 ]
As regards the other letters, they differ. Some derive from quadriliteral final-weak
[verbs], just as some of the verbs in the preceding chapter derive from triliteral final-weak verbs 7
[426427]. The fin [letter] is dropped and doubling takes its place. Example :: UL ’NIK 1
YUVZIN TAR:MA’DIY ol anig yiizin tarmadi “He scratched his [face]. This is a final-weak |
verb because in the imperative one says: TAR-:MA’ tarma meaning “Scratch!” with four conso- a
nants [dawat al-arba‘a (‘“final-weak”) literally means “having four”]. When the verb is made 7
doubly transitive one says: "UL*ANY TARMATIY ol ani tarmatti meaning ‘“‘He had him scratch d
him.” The alif which was in the final-weak form drops in favor of ta’. Imperative: TARMAT: =
tarmat “Have him scratch!”—the alif drops in favor of ta’ as you see. 0 ::’UL’ANY BAT- =
DA’DIY ol ani baydadi “He tripped him.” Imperative: BAI'DA’ bayda “Trip!” It is a final- |
weak verb, When it is made doubly transitive, one says: "UL ’ANIK ’ADA’QIN BAT-DATIY |
ol anig adaqin baydatti “He ordered him to be tripped.” The alif drops in favor of a’. Impera- |
tive: BAT-DAT: baydat “Order him to be tripped.” |
This is the first aspect. :
Some derive from nouns. Example :: |
[II. 293/365] 445 |
"UL BUVZUK QAR.SATIY ol bdziig qarSatti ‘He had the cloth measured in spans”’;! its root is: S|
QARIS garis meaning “span (Sibr).” i: °UL’ANIY QURQUTY ol ani gorqitti “He frightened
(xawwafa) him”; its root is: QURQUN:J gorquné. :: UL YYRIK *AT-LATIY ol yérig aylatti
“He emptied (axla) the place”; its root is: *AD-LA’Q: YIYR: aylaq yér meaning “An empty :
(xalin) place.” The final letter in nouns of this type drops in favor of ta’, :
Most of the verbs in this chapter are doubly transitive, one of the agents ordering and
the other carrying out the action, as we have explained. Some are intransitive final-weak verbs
to which is added the ta’ of transitivity. Example :: "AT: SAM-RIYDIY at simridi “The horse 7
put on fat”; then: ’AR- ’ATIN SAM-RITY dr atin simritti “The man fattened the horse.”
i} NA’NK ’UK-LIYDIY niin iiklidi “The thing grew in quantity”;? then: ’AR NA’NKNY(YN)? :
°UK-LITIY ar nanin iiklitti “The man increased his wealth.” 0 ;
The last aspect is that it be independent and have none of these meanings. Example 7
i: 7AR: TAWRATIY 4r tawratti “The man hurried”;* :: °UPLA’N- ;ULTATIY ovlan ulyatti {
“The boy (or other) grewup.”* 0 7

YAT- yat A type of divination (kahana) using special stones with which one brings on yat
rain, wind, etc. It is well known among them. I myself witnessed it in Yayma where it was
performed to put out a fire that had broken out.

Snow fell in the summer, by the leave of God Most High, and put out the fire in my presence.

: YAL-MA’ yalma The yalmaq [a padded raincoat]. The Persians took it from the yalma
: Turks and said yalmah; then the Arabs took it from the Persians and said yalmaq, changing the
1 ha’ to qaf, just as they say xandaq (‘‘ditch”) from [Persian] kandah 0 and yaraq (“bracelet’’)
: from [Persian] yarah. Even Dii-t-Rumma mentions this word, in his verse: ka-annahu muta-
| qabbi yalmaqin (“As though he were robed in a yalmaq’). It was Arabized. No one can say
1 that the Turks took this word from the Persians, because I heard it from crude (ajlaf) Turks on
the farthest frontiers; also, they have a greater need for raincoats (mamtar) than other people,
| since rain and snow are more plentiful in their lands.

YATMURJIL YIYR- yaymuréil yér “A place where there is much rain (yakturu fihi yaymuréil |
Lmatar).” |
This is a rule, that when anything follows continuously upon a thing, you suffix jim and G |

lam to the noun from which the action originates.
[IL 43/56] 469 |
|

It then becomes an adjectival (sifa) of that thing. For example, a “windy place (mirwah [de- 1
fined] )” is: TUBIYJIL YIYR- tipiéil yér; TUBIY tiipi is the word for “wind (rih),” and the jim |
and lam are suffixed to it making it an adjectival indicating abundance (wasf4” li-Lkatra). “A |
man who is always sick (mimrad)” is called: "TYK-JIL- igéil. However, this rule is rarely applied. : |
1

End of the Book of Initial Weak Nouns |

7 “He shared with me, and we knew one another; he helped me sell my goods; but he agreed with
2 the host (rabb al-bayt), and hid from me my colt.” 0  BISTA’ bista is the word for a person
_ who acts as host (mudif) to merchants. The merchant alights at his house, and he sells his goods,
herds his sheep, and hosts him as long as he remains. When the guest leaves, he takes one out
of twenty of the sheep. This is the custom of the Tuxsi, Yayma, and Cigil; I have witnessed them
: doing this. YARASUVR YARSM’Q yaraSiir yarasmaq. 0

i: “AR: BJA’K YANUVDIY @r bié’k yanidi “The man honed (Sahida) the knife (or yanu- |
sword, or the like).” This is when he passes it over his hand after he has sharpened (sanna) it.

:: °URA’TUT: YANIYDIY urayut yenidi “The woman gave birth (waladat, wada‘at yeni- {
hamlaha).” YINIYR YANIYMA’K yenir yenimak. This is only said of a woman; for other G |
animals, the name of the young is taken, and the particle: LA’DIY -ladi is suffixed to it in order |
to express giving birth (nitaj). Example, for a cow: ’"INKA’K BUZA’TUVLA’DIY ingak buzayi- |
Tadi “the cow bore a calf”; BUZATTUV buzayu is “calf,” and to it was suffixed: LA’DIY -ladi, 7
thus making it a verb for giving birth. A “chick” is called: BALA’ bala; to say, “The bird bore |
young,” you say: QUS: BALA’LA’DIY qué balaladi. This is the rule, except for a mare. A |
“colt” is called: QULUN: qulun; to say ‘‘The mare gave birth,” you say: QIS-RQ QUL:NA’DIY |
qisraq quinadi; it ought to have been: QULUN-LA’DIY *qulunladi, but this is not said, because |
niin and lam are articulated at the same point and alternate with one another, and so it is light- |
ened. :: ’AR-S-LA’N ’ANVK-LA’DIY arslan 4nikladi “The lioness (or other) bore a cub.”
The etymology of the phrase: "URA’TUT: YANIYDIY urayut yenidi has two pos- |
sible explanations. One is that it derives from the phrase: YANIK NA’NK yenik nay meaning
“a light (xafif) thing,” since when she gives birth she is “lightened (xaffat)” and so one says: |
YANIY-DIY yenidi. The nin has kasr (I) but damm (U) is the rule for this type. The other
explanation is that it derives from: YIYN: yén meaning “body (badan)”—as though, when a |
woman gives birth, she expells |
[UT. 65/92] 483 |
a body (jasad) from herself. Both of these explanations are plausible.

a 203
: :: YUW:LUN-DY NA’NK = yuwlundi nin “The thing rolled (tadahraja).” YUW:- yuwlun-
s LUNUVR YUWLNM’Q yuwlunir yuwlunmaq. 0
: i: “AR: TA’M.QA’ YARMAN-DIY r tama yarmandi “The man scaled (tasallaga) the yarman-
: wall (or other).”” YAR-MANUVR: YARMANM’Q yarmaniir yarmanmagq.
: Rule. When any biliteral verb which is transitive has lam suffixed to it, it becomes G
: intransitive or passive, as was shown above. 0
When nun is suffixed to a transitive verb it becomes intransitive. This agrees with
Arabic, since in Arabic you say fataha baba (‘He opened the door”), which is transitive; then
infataha |-babu (‘The door opened’’), which is intransitive. When niin is added the verb changes
: from transitive to intransitive. Again, you say kasara” r-rajulu %-Say’a (“The man broke the
thing”); then inkasara (“It broke’’), which is intransitive.
In this language you say: ’AR- TKUVN YAZ.-DIY 4r tiigiin yazdi meaning “The man
untied (halla) the knot’; then you add niin and say: TKUVN YAZLIN.-DIY [sic] tiigiin yazindi
: meaning “The knot came loose (inhallat).’’? The verb becomes intransitive with the addition of
: nun. 0
With lam, you say: TUKUVN YAZIL-DIY tiigtin yazildi meaning ‘“‘the knot was untied
(hullat)”® ; this also means “It came loose (inhallat)”; then nian may be combined with lam, thus:
: YAZ:LIN:DIY yazlindi meaning ‘The knot came untied by itself (inhallat bi-tab‘ihi).” Similarly,
[UL 78/112] 491
: one says: “AR: TUBIQ YUWDIY 4r topiq yuwdi “The man rolled (dahraja) the ball”; then:
TUBIQ YUWUL:DIY topiq yuwuldi “The ball was rolled (duhrijat).”! Then niin may be added
: to it, thus: YUW-LUN-DIY yuwlundi meaning “It rolled by itself (tadahraja bi-tab‘ihi).” Before
the niin was added to the lam the verb was intransitive in two respects. One is that the action
4 was performed by an unknown or absent agent; in this case the verb acts intransitively, and when
| nin is combined with it, the verb becomes intransitive without anything else performing the
| action upon it [ie. middle]. As in the above example: YUW-:DIY yuwdi means “He rolled
F (dahraja)”—a biliteral transitive verb; when lam is added, thus: YUWUL:DIY yuwuldi it means
: “It was rolled by the agency of something else (duhrija bi-fi‘l yayrihi)” [i.e. passive] ; or else “It
: rolled by itself (tadahraja bi-nafsihi)” [i.e. middle]. The verb becomes triliteral and intransitive
in two respects. And when niin and lam are combined, thus: YUW:LUN:DIY yuwlundi it means
“Tt rolled by itself (tadahraja bi-nafsihi).” The verb becomes quadriliteral and intransitive,
2 progressing from biliteral to triliteral, and from triliteral to quadriliteral.
7 Most of the rules were given in the Book of Sound Words [279ff.]. All of these verbs
a are used in the imperative, the negative, and so on, according to the principles that have been
a given, 0
End of the Quadriliteral Chapters 0

ha’ is in pause.

MA’H mah A particle meaning “here (hunaka)” or “‘take it (xud),”’ said when handing mah
something over.
Initial Weak
YA’H yah A particle meaning “‘yes (na‘am).” This is used as a reply to someone who yah
says: ’AT- TUT: at tut meaning “Take the horse,’ and the one who replies says: YA’H: yah
meaning “Yes,”
The lin letters drop from these words in rapid speech. :: T: TAH: TAH: tah tah, J: G
JUH: JUH: éoh Goh, K: KAH- KAH- kih kaéh, M: MAH- mah, [Y:] YAH- yah. For this reason
we included them in the chapter of defective words (bab al-manqiis), since the lin letter is absent
(yungqagu) from them in speech, though not in writing.
The ha’ in these cases is the ha’ of pause (ha’ istiraha), as in the verses: fa-bi-hudahumu
qtadih ([Q.6:90] “So follow their guidance’’), and: lam yatasannah ({(Q.2:259] “it has not
spoiled”), The ha’ is in pause in these cases.
; A radical (asli) ha’ does not exist in genuine Turkic, except in the phrase: ’AR- ’H-
; LADY 4r ihladi meaning “The man hiccupped (axada r-rajula r-rabwu)”—but this is onomato-
poeic for the sound that arises from the breast [cf. 31 iq]; and the word for “owl (bam)”:
; "UVHIY iihi—even here, the genuine Turks
[II 84/118] 494
: call it: "UVKIY tigi with kaf. The ha’ is found in the speech of Khotan and Kunzk, since they D
: are not Turkic, but are settlers (nazil) in the lands of the Turks. 0

as far as Upper Sin, prefer damma (U). The Oyuz, Qiféaq, and Suvarin, as far as Rim, prefer

YA’Y: yay “Spring (rabi‘).” Proverb [= 352-353 balgiir-]: KUVZ KALIKY YA’YN yay
BAL:KUVLUK kiiz kaligi yayin balgiiliig 0 ‘The way autumn will come is apparent at the P
coming of spring.” This is coined about something whose end result is known from the very
beginning. Verse:

YA’Y- KURKINK’ ’INAN-MA’ yay kérking inanma v
SUWLAR ’UZ’ TYANM’ suwlar iizi tayanma
*ASIZ-LILIK: ’ANUN:MA’ esizlikig anunma
TILDA’ JIQA’R: ’DKUV SUVZ tilda Cigar ddgii s6z
“Do not depend on spring (or hope for good from its blossoms and bright colors); do not lean on
water (for he who depends on taking pleasure from the good things of this world and from
spring breezes is like one who leans on water); do not prepare yourself for evil; but bring from
your tongue fine words (with which people will be pleased).”

Rule. We have called this and other chapters “defective (mangiis)” since the medial G
lin letter may drop from the word, rendering it biliteral in pronunciation, though not in writing;
in its written form the letters remain sound,

{IIL 121/161] 516
Example: The “forearm (‘adud)” is called: QUVL: q6l. Then you say: "NIK QULIY! ’L-DIY
anig golin aldi meaning “‘He grasped his arm.”’ It has become like the word for “‘slave”: QUL qul
since the vav has dropped out. Similarly: YIYL yél “wind (nasim)”; in the course of speech you
say: "NIK YALIY KUVJ-LUK anig yeli kii¢liig “His wind (rik) is strong,” meaning that he has
strength. It has become like the word for “‘year’: YIL yil. This is the rule for all nouns and
verbs in the “deficient” chapter. Notice how this agrees with Arabic. For example, the Arabic
verbs: zana, yazinu, imperative: zin (“adorn”); kala, yakilu, kil (“measure”); the fin letter
has dropped out in each case. But in this language [Turkic] there is a single rule for both nouns
and verbs, due to its encompassing nature (lisi‘atiha), whereas in Arabic this rule holds good
only for verbs, not for nouns. Learn it!

TUVRUR turur A verb in the aorist (mustaqbal). It has no preterite (madi) and no —_(tirur)
infinitive, like the Arabic words yada‘u (‘the lets”) and yadaru (“he lets”). 0. It denotes the
existence or position of a thing at the time it is mentioned (yunbi’u ‘an garar a8-Say’ wa-wujiidihi |
makanahu fi hala dikrihi iyyahu). Thus :: ’UL ’AWDA’ TRUVR. ol awda turir “He is present |
(hadir) in the house.” This does not imply “standing.” ::’AR- SUKAL- TRUVR &r sokal turdir |
“{The man] is sick (annahu marid).” This does not imply “standing.” 0

Rule. This type of verb is formed from the triliteral nouns in this book with various G |
meanings: |
1) With the meaning that the thing became with him or accompanied him. Example:: |
“AR: TAYAQLANDY Gr tayaqlandi “The man became the owner of a staff.” :: SUVT QAYUQ.
LANDY sit qayaqlandi ‘The pellicle appeared on the milk (sarat al-laban ma‘a d-duwaya).” |
)
2) With the meaning that one reckons the thing to belong to the category of the noun. |
Example:: ’AR- ’ATII QIYZ-LAN-DIY 4r atiy qizlandi “The man reckoned the horse to be |
expensive (‘adda... yaliya),” :: "UL’ANY QIYZLAN-DIY ol ani qizlandi |
|
[I 148/198] 532 |
|
“He counted the girl as one of his daughters (‘adda Ljariya min jumla banatihi)” [Lit. “He con-
sidered her as a daughter.”’]. |
3) As a verb whose agent is not named. Example:: TAWA’R JUVI-LAN-DIY tawar
éuylandi “The goods were tied up (ruzima).” :: BUVZ JIUP-LANDY béz éiylandi “The cloth
(or other) was measured off in cubits (duri‘a).”
4) As a verb indicating similarity to the noun. Example:: ’AR- TAYUQLANDY ar
tayuqlandi ‘‘The man dressed up asa dandy,” etc.
Two other categories may be mentioned here by analogy:
1) When the speaker means to say that [the subject] accompanies the noun or is its
owner or master. Example:: TA’T- TIYT-LANDIY tay titlandi “The mountain was covered with
pines (sara . . . ma‘a 3ajar as-sanawbar).” 0 :: 7AR- QA’B-LAN-DY 4r gaplandi “The man
became owner or master of a wineskin (sahib ziqq wa-malikahu).”
ee
531 3MS. mimma ja‘ala Lasl, read with EP: mimma ja’a ‘ala Lasl; cf. 516:9. |

===== part 2 page 260 =====
253
2) With the meaning that he reckons the thing to belong to the category of the noun.
Example:: "UL NA’NKNIY QIYZ-LAN-DY ol ninni qizlandi ‘He reckoned the thing expensive”
—and others of that sort that we have explained.
You will not go wrong if you form the remaining cases by analogy with these, and
pronounce them in the meanings mentioned, since there is nothing to prevent any noun from
being declined by adding the suffix in question. However, not every noun in all the dialects may
be formed into a verb in this way. It is not permitted, for example, to say: "AR- SA’W-LAN.-
DIY ar *sawlandi to mean “The man coined a proverb (daraba... matal4")”; or: AR JA’W--
LAN-DIY 4r *éawlandi to mean “He became famous (sara lahu sit). But all of these are per-
mitted on the basis of analogy, so no one will blame you if you use them.

SA’ sa A particle meaning “you (anta).” Thus :: SA’ "AYUR MAN sa ayur min “I sa
say to you (aqiiluka).” The alif is an alternant of the niin of SAN sin or else is the result of a

SUV sii “Army or troops (jund).” Proverb: ’TA’TQA’ "UBKAILA’B- SUVK’ SUVZ~ sii

SUV KAL-DY YIYR: YABA’ sii kaldi yér yapa Vv
QR’R QR op? qazar garliy tay tapa (?)
QAJTY ’ANKAR ’AL-B’AYA’ qaécti anar alp apa

“The army came and covered the land’s horizon; [digging (?)] at the snow-covered mountains

SIY -si A particle for the possessive construct (idafa) suffixed to nouns of which the “si
final letter is unvowelled; when it is vowelled, then a ya’ [i.e. -i] is sufficient. Example :: ’ATA’ G
ata “Father”—the [final] alif is unvowelled, and so with the possessive ending it is: ’ANIK
*ATA’SIY anig atasi “His father (abahu).” :: ANA’ ana “Mother.” ’ANIK: ’ANA’SIY anig anasi
[His mother..] here too the [final] alif is unvowelled. One cannot say: ’ANIK ’ATA’YIY
anig *atayi with two yd’s, since then there would come together
[Ul 157/210] 587
three lin letters. For this reason the medial one is replaced by a sin so that the pronunciation
is euphonious and the lin [letter] disappears. 0
Examples of the vowelled: ’UTUL- oyul ‘Son (ibn).” :: ’ANIK "UPLIY anig oli
“His son (ibnuhu).” The sin is not used here because the lam is vowelled in the construct. ::
‘AT: at “Horse (faras).” :: "ANIK ’ATIY anig ati “His horse (farasuhu).” The ta’ is vowelled so
one does not require the sin.
This is the rule for all nouns in all of the dialects. The final letter of the word is exam-
ined as to whether it is vowelled or not and the construct is build thereon.

K

K’ ka An adverbial particle meaning “to (ila).” It is suffixed to nouns having kaf, -ka
rikka, or imala. Thus :: "UL ’AFKA’ BAR-DIY ol awki bardi “He went to (ila) the house.” It
can [also] have the meaning “into (fi).””. Thus :: UL ’AFKA’ KIR-DIY ol dwk& kirdi “He went
into (fi) the house.” It can [also] have the meaning of the prepositional lam. Thus :: UL
TAR KA’ KIR-DIY ol tarké kirdi “He entered paid employment (daxala Lujra).”

KUV kii Reknown (sit) among the people.” Hence :: KUVLUK BIL-KA’ killiig bilga kii
“A famous (dii sit) scholar.”

L

LA’ la A particle used by the Oyuz to indicate that an action has been verified (tahaq- laD
quq) or completed (tamam). It is attached to the end of verbs. Thus :: "UL BAR-:DIY LA’ ol
bardi la ‘‘He went and his going has been verified (tahagqqaqa).” :: UL KAL-DIY LA’ ol kaldi la
“He came and his coming has been verified.” There is involved here a degree of denying what
the person addressed has said, implying that he does not know about the actual occurrence
of the event. The Tiirks do not use this.

M

MA’ -ma A negative (nahy) particle, suffixed to the imperative form of verbs. Thus :: -ma
QIL qil “Do!” QIL-MA’ qilma “Don’t do! (1a taf‘al).” This is the rule for negation of all verbal
stems, whether simple or augmented.

MA’ ma A particle meaning “Take it (xud).”” You say: MA’ ma to mean “Here, take ma
it! (ud wa-haka).” It may be pronounced with ha’ in place of alif: MAH- mah as one says [in
Arabic] abriya, habriya (‘pellicle’), or araga, haraqa (“to pour”). 0

MA’ ma Onomatopoeic for the voice of sheep and lambs. This agrees with the Arabic
in the verse of
[HL 160/216] 539
Di ’r-Rumma: bismi ’l-ma’i mabyumu (‘Bleated to with the word ‘ma’.”). 0

MUV -mu/-mii Interrogative particle, attached to the end of both nouns and verbs. -mu/mii
Thus :: BUV ’AT-MUV bu at mu “Is (a-) this a horse?” :: BUV ’IT-MUV bu it mu “Is this a
dog?” :: BAR-DINKMUV bardin, mu “Did you go?”” KAL‘DINKMUV Kildin, mii “Did you
come?”

Some of the Oyuz differ from the Turks in the third person interrogative of the verb; D
they put a kasra on the mim and make the waw a ya’ asin: "UL BAR:DIYMIY ol bardi mi
“Did he go?” They agree with the Turks in regard to nouns; thus :: BUV ’AT: SANIK- MUV
bu at sinig mii “Is this horse yours?” Likewise: BUV ’UTUL- SANIK MUV bu oyul sinig mii
“Is this child yours?”

JWIY @iwi Name of a party (hizb) of jinn. The Turks maintain that when two groups tiwi
do battle, the jinn who dwell in their respective lands fight each other beforehand, out of loyalty
to the human rulers of their two lands. Whichever of them is victorious, victory comes to the
ruler of that one’s land on the morrow; but whichever of them is defeated during the night,

the king in whose country that party of the jinn dwell. The armies of the Turks shield them-
selves on the eve of battle and keep to their tents, to protect themselves from the arrows of the
jinn. This is a well-known belief among them. 0

TIKIY tiki (tegi ?) “A rustling noise (dawi) heard during the night.” The Turks claim tiki
that the spirits of the dead gather one night each year, go into the settlements (amsar) where _(tegi?)
they had passed their lives, and visit their families (taziiru ahaliha). Whoever chances upon this
noise at night dies. This is a well-known belief among the Turks. The word has kasra (I) on the
ta’, but in my opinion nasb (A [thus tdgi ?]) would be better, since you say of a woman who
comes to visit her family after she has been given to her husband: TAK-DIY KAL-DIY tagdi
kaldi meaning ‘She came visiting (ja’at za’irat®).” 0

:: QIYZ SAJIN TARA’DIY iz saéin taradi ‘The girl combed (imta¥atat) her hair.” :: tara-
*ANIK SUVSIN TARA’DIY anig siisin taradi “He dispersed (farraqa) his troops.” Its root-form
is: TAR-DIY tardi meaning “‘to disperse (tafriq)” a thing [524 tar-]. Notice that they are
identical in the aorist; thus :: TAR-DIY tardi ‘He dispersed,’ TARA’R: tarar and TARA’DIY
TARAYR: taradi, tarar. This rule is not consistent for all verbs; however the aorist is identical in G
[some] biliteral and triliteral [verbs] but the preterite differs.1 Example :: ’AR- ’AR-T ’AS-DIY
‘dr art aSdi “The man crossed the mountain pass”; :: "UL ’AS ’ASA’DIY ol a8 a8adi “He ate the
food”; the aorist of both is: ’ASA’R: a8ar—the identical form, :: UL SUWQA’ QAN-DIY ol
suwga qandi “He had his fill of the water’’—this is the perfect; then: QANA’R-: ganar “He is
satisfied”; QAN-M’Q qanmaq, :: ’UL ’ATIN: QANA’DIY ol atin qanadi “He opened the vein
of his horse”; then: QANA’R- qanar—the same form as above; QANAMA’MA’Q [sic] qanamaq.
They agree in the aorist but differ in the preterite and infinitive. 0

:: UPLA’N SIE-TA’DIY ovylan siytadi “The baby (or other) cried (baka).” SIT:TA’R siyta-
SIT-TA’MA’Q siytar siytamaq. There is a variant with xa’ [i.e. sixta-]. This is like Arabic yumar, D
xumar (“crowd”) and yaddar, xattar (“disloyal”). This is permitted [in Turkic] with verbs but G
not with nouns; thus it is not permitted to say: SIXIT *sixit for ‘crying (buka’)” instead of
Yayn [i.e. 179 siyit].

has been elided because of the requirement of the verse. This is permited in Oyuz and Qiftaq D
dialect, since they elide the medial yayn of both nouns and verbs.!

Rule. The imperative in this chapter is composed of four letters and for this reason we G
have called it quadriliteral, Example :: ’AT- TUI-RA’ St toyra “Cut the meat”; :: "AWKA’
°Ul- RA’ iiwkd uyra “Got home.” If the speaker wishes, he may say: TUTRATIL ’UrRATIL
toyrayil, uyrayil.
The third person imperative is: TUCRA’SUVN: toyrasiin “Let him cut”; UTRA’-
SUVN: uyrasiin “Let him go.”
Negative imperative: one adds mim and alif to the imperative stem. Example ::
| °UPRA’MA’ uyrama “Don’t go”; :: TUTRA’MA’ toyrama “Don’t cut.” 0 The third person
prohibitive is: "UTRA’MA’SUVN: uyramasin “Let him not go”; TUTRA’MA’SUVN toyramasiin

310
To express the future tense (sawfa yaf‘alu) one adds to the imperative stem ayn alif
ya’ when the word has iba‘ or yayn, or kaf instead of yayn in words having kaf or rikka. Exam-
ple :: MAN ’AT- TUPRA’T'A’Y- MAN: min 4t toyrayay man “I shall cut the meat”; MAN
*ANKAR-: ’UPRATA’Y- MAN min anar uyrayay miin “I shall go to him.” This is the rule for
all verbs, in this chapter and in others.
{IIl. 231/312] 581
The explanation of this is that, in the Arabic dialects, meanings are indicated by pre-
fixes, such as the imperfect prefixes, the interrogative prefix [a-], the sin of the future tense |
[sa-], the disjunctive alifs, the conjunctive waw and the like.
In the Turkic dialects, however, the stem remains fixed and suffixes are added to the
end, as has been observed [8-9,281]. The same obtains for the active participles. The participial
marker in Arabic is an infix as in: yafir (“forgiving’’), yaffar (‘“much-forgiving’”), yafur (‘‘much-
forgiving’). But in this language, these markers are placed after the entire word, as we shall
explain.
The negative aorist: a sin is added to the negative suffix; thus :: "UL ’AT- TUI-RA’-
MA’S: ol &t toyramas “He does not cut the meat.” 0  :: UL SANKA’’UPRA’MA’S ol sana
uyramas “He does not go to you.”
The prohibitive of the [3rd person] negative :: "UL ’AT- TUTRA’MA’SUVN: ol at
toyramastn “He must not cut the meat.” :: UL ’ANKA’R: ’URA’MA’SUVN: ol anar uyrama-
sun “Let him not go to him!”
The plural: This rule has been given [289], which is that the nasal kaf is added to the
singular imperative stem according to the rule of Qiféaq and Oyuz. Thus :: "AT: TUTRA’NK D
at toyran “Cut [pl.] the meat!” :: ’AFKA’ "UPRA’NK awka uyray “Go [pl.] home!” This
is a constant rule; however, the Turks employ the nasal kaf for the second person singular for
one who is respected or aged; so that it is permissible to say for a collective: TUTRA’NK-LA’R:
toyranlar, *UTRA’NK-LA’R uyranjar. Were it not for this meaning, the remaining Turks could
not combine these two suffixes of the plural, since they stand for one another. The Oyuz and
Qiféaq employ the first method which is according to rule.
The sound participle in this chapter is thus ::
[HII 232/314] 582
*AT- TUPRA'TUVJIY at toyrayuéi meaning ‘‘One who cuts the meat.” :: ’AFKA’ UTRATUV-
JIY awk uyrayuéi “One who goes home.” In the dialect of the Oyuz and Qiféaq, the nomadic D
peoples who are related to them, and the Suvarin:! TUIRA’DA’JIY toyradadi, "UTRA’DAJIY
uyradaéi. This rule has been discussed [290-293].
582 MS. Savarin.

The participle describing the continuity of the action :: TUT-RA’TA’N toyrayan mean-
ing “One who often cuts”; ’UTRA’I”N uyrayan “One who often goes.” They all follow this rule.
The participle describing that one desires to perform the action. One adds Yayn to the
isba‘ liquids or words having ‘yayn, and kf in the opposite case [i.e., palatal], plus sin and qaf
or kaf. Ex. :: (UL ’AT- TUCRAI-SA’Q ’UL ol at toyraysaq ol “He wants to cut the meat.”
And: °UL TAVAYKA’ YUK YUK-LAK-SA’K: ’UL ol tevéki yiik yiiklagsk ol “He wants to load
(haml) the camel.” However, this form is not used in this chapter of final-weak verbs as much
as in the other chapters.
The participle describing that one is about to perform the action and intends to do so
but has not yet accomplished it. Ex. :: ’UL’AT TUPRAI-LY ’UL ol at toyrayli ol “He intends
to cut the meat and is about to do so.” :: "UL SANKA’ UPRAI-LY ’UL ol sana uyrayli ol “He
is intending to go to you and is about to do so.” The remainder follow this rule.
The participle describing that one ought to perform that. Ex. :: "UL ’AT- TUPRI-
LIQ [sic] "AR:DIY ol at toyrayulug 4rdi “He should have cut the meat.” And: ’UL SANK’
*UTRAT-LIQ ’AR-DIY ol sana uyrayulug ardi “He should have gone to you.”
In the dialect of Oyuz and Qiféaq, sin is used instead of lam. Ex. :: ’UL’AT- TUTRT.-
SAIQ.? ’R-DIY ol &t toyraysaq drdi “He should have cut the meat.” :: UL SANK’ ’UPRAT-- D
SAIQ? ’AR-DIY ol sana wyraysaq ardi.
The majority pronounce it following the rule .

of the Turks. 0

is not cutting the meat”; "UL YUK YUK-LA’MADAJIY TURUVR. ol yiik yiiklimadaéi turir
“He is not one who is loading.” The remainder follow this rule. In another dialect [sic—?]: D
TU RA’MAI-LIY ’UL YUK-LA’MAK-LY ’UL toyramayli ol, yiiklamigli ol—mim and yayn?
along with lam and ya’ are added to the imperative stem with iba‘ letters, or kaf [instead of
Yayn] in the opposite case.

iryamis yiyaé ‘A shaken tree.” This follows the rule of the sound verbs.

Know that I mention the preterite, the aorist and the infinitive because the preterite i
sometimes differs from the aorist in its medial vowelling and sometimes is identical with it, and I
this cannot be known by a rule. Example :: YAYR: QUR-I’DIY yer quryadi “The ground was |
dry”; QUR-TA’R QURI'A’MA’Q quryar quryamaq—the preterite agrees with aorist and infini- |

{IlIl. 235/318] 584 \
“He strung the bow”; QURA’R: QURMA’Q qurar qurmaq—here it does not agree.! The differ- (

This chapter has several aspects.
One is to be a verb compounded of a biliteral or a defective triliteral noun, with the
meaning that the agent performs an action associated with the object named. Example :: ’AR-
'UQ: KAZLA’DIY 4r oq kazlidi “The man repaired the notch of his arrow”; its root is: KAZ-
kaz the word for “notch” to which was added the suffix: LA’DIY -ladi making it a verb. ::
*AR: TAWA’R: KIZLA’DIY ar tawar kizladi “The man hid the goods (or other)”; its root is:
KIZ. kiz a noun [164 “box’’]. In the defective [category] :: BULIT- QARLA’DIY bulit qarladi
“The clouds brought forth snow”; :: QUTUS YAILA’DIY qoyus yayladi “(The man) oiled the
leather”’—the roots are: QA’R: gar “snow” and: YA: yay “oil”; the weak letter was elided for
lightening. |
When the noun is a sound triliteral, an alif suffices, and there is no need to combine it
with lam. Example :: YASIN- ya’in “lightning”; then :: YASIN YASNA’DIY yagin yasnadi
“The lightning flashed.” :: QIS-RA’Q QULNA’DIY qisraq quinadi “The mare foaled” [cf. 203
qulun]. :: QIS-RAQ YILNA’DIY qisraq yelnadi “The mare was swollen in the udders at giving
birth” [cf. 454 yelin]. Since the noun is sound, just an alif without a lam suffices to make it a
complete verb.
Sound quadriliteral nouns may also be included in this chapter. The final letter is
dropped, making it a triliteral; then, when it is combined with alif, it [again] becomes quadri-
literal. Example :: YUM-SA’Q yumSaq “‘something soft”; then :: YUM-SA’DIY yumiadi “(The

:: SUVW- BUL-TA’DIY siiw bulyadi “The water was muddy”; its root is: BULIA’(N)YUQ-

to become verbs. Or else these nouns may be derived (ma’xiid) from verbs through suffixation;
in my opinion this is more correct, because nouns are derived (yustanbatu) from verbs. Example
:: SUVD-TIY siudti “He spit”; then :: SUDUQ. sudug “‘spittle.” :: SIDTIY sidti “He urinated”;
then: SIDUK: sidiik “urine.” 0 :: ’AT> BIS:DIY &t biSdi “The meat cooked”; then: BISIT
’AT- bi8iy At “cooked [meat].” This excellence does not belong to any other language but
Arabic and Turkic.
The other aspect is that it be a root-category based on itself alone without any of these
meanings. Example :: "AR: YITA’J- IRTA’DIY ar yiyaé iryadi “The man shook the tree.” ::
AR: YIPLA’DIY ar yiyladi “The man wept.” :: AR: ’IYS-QA’’UPRA’DY 4r i8qa uyradi “The
man went to work.” 0
The way to express mutuality of the action between two or more, as with the mufa‘ala
pattern in Arabic, is to drop the weak letter in final position and add Sin in its place. Example ::
°UL MANIK BIRLA’ YII'A’J- ’IR-TAS-DIY ol minig birlé yiyaé iryaddi “He shook the tree with
me, either helping or competing.” :: KISY BARJA’ YICLAS-DIY ki8i baréa yiyla8di “The
people [all] wept.” They are all formed according to this rule.
To express that the agent pretends to do the action but does not really do it, the weak
letter is again dropped and in its place is added mim combined with sin and niin. Example ::
?UL SANK’ ’UrRAM-SIN-DIY ol sana uyramsindi “He pretended to go to you but did not really
do so.” :: UL YUK YUK-LAM-SIN-DIY ol yiik yiiklimsindi “He pretended to load but was
not really loading.”
Rules other than these have been given in the Book of Sound Words and may be re-
ferred to there [279-304]. All the dialects follow these rules, and they are not contradicted,
God willing. 0

Qatun Sini is the name of a place, being a city between Tangut and Sin. The two of them were
warring, and Tangut routed the people of Qatun Sini: “(The people of) Qatun Sini shouted
(saxaba) and warred against (the people of) Tangut and their emir, until their blood flowed like
: gurgling (xarir) water, and their necks milked red water (meaning blood).”

i: 7R- KDIZ KUYA’LA’DIY ar kidiz kiiyalidi “The man removed the moth-worms _ kiiyala- /
(sits) from the felt (or other) (by shaking them off and driving’ them from it).” KUY’LA’R

The rule in this chapter is as we explained in the previous chapter [580-584]. G
This chapter revolves around various aspects:
1) To a final weak triliteral noun is added: LA’DIY -ladi with the meaning that the
agent performs an action associated with the object named. Example :: ’AR- TUVN: QARA’-
LA’DIY ar ton qaraladi “The man blackened the garment.” :: YYR: QARIYLA’DIY yer qariladi
“The man measured the ground in cubits.” 0
2) When LA’DIY -ladi is added to nouns the meaning is that one connects or relates
(nasaba) the person to the thing named. Example :: "UL ’ARIK ’AQIYLA’DIY ol drig aqiladi
“He connected the man to generosity.” :: "UL ’ANY QARIYLA’DIY ol ani qariladi “He con-
nected him to old age.”
3) It is a verb based on itself alone without intending any of these meanings. Example
[UL 243/329] 589
"UL ANIY QUJIYLA’DY ol ani qiéiladi “He tickled him (ahakka. . . fi mawdi‘ ad-dibh).” +:
*AS-YA’K TUMIYLA’DIY yak tiimiladi [“The donkey trotted”].1_ 0
End of the Quadriliteral Chapters

i: UL ’ANY YAI-RINLA’DIY° ol ani yarinladi ‘He struck him on the shoulder- _yarinla-

Rule. The imperative in this chapter has five letters [consonantal graphemes]. Exam- G
ple :: MANIY ’AIIR-LA’ mini ayirla “Honor me”; :: MANK? ’AT: BAT'IS-LA’ manga at bayisla
“Present a horse tome.” 0
As for the negative imperative, the negative aorist, the second person, the active
participles and their adjectivals, the passive participle, the nouns of place and time and instru-
ment—these are formed regularly according to the rules of the preceding chapters. 0
When a true passive (majhiil) is desired in this chapter one adds a lam to the imperative
stem, thus producing two lams, Example :: AR: ATIRLAL-DIY ir ayirlaldi “The man was
honored (ukrima)”; :: AT: BATISLAL-DIY at bayi8laldi “The horse was presented (wuhiba).”
It is also permitted to use niin instead of lam. Example :: AT- BATISLAN-DIY at bayi8landi
“The horse was presented”;
[III 254/344] 595
*AR: ’ATIRLAN-DY @r ayirlandi “The man was honored.’”? However, the first one is used more.
You have learned that the mutual action of the verb between two is indicated by
adding a Sin; this is according to rule.
There are six aspects to this chapter.
1) It is composed from a sound triliteral noun. Example :: QABUI KIRT-LA’DIY
qapuy kiritlédi “He locked the door”; its root is: KIRIT: kirit “key” and to it was added:
LA’DIY -ladi making it a verb. :: "UL’ANY QASIILA’DIY ol ani qasiyladi “He punched him
on the jaw”; the noun is: QASII' qasiy “jaw” and to it was added: LA’DIY -ladi making it a
verb. This chapter revolves around this in all meanings.
| 2) It has the meaning that the man, or other, is considered to belong to the class of
the word mentioned. Example :: UL’ ANY ’UUZLA’DIY ol ani oyuzladi “He connected him
to the Oyuz and considered him among them.” :: UL ’ANIY JIKILA’DIY ol ani éigilladi “He
| considered him a Cigili and connected him to them.” The same for anything to which a man is
| connected. Example :: ’UL’ANY SARAN-LA’DIY ol ani saranladi “He considered him a miser
and connected him with miserliness.” This is like the Arabic zallamtahu and fassaqtahu, mean-
ing “you connected him with injustice (zulm) and immorality (fisq).”
3) It has the meaning that he struck him on the body member represented by the
mentioned word. Example :: "UL ’ANY QARIN-LA’DIY ol ani qarinladi “He ‘bellied’ him—
meaning he struck him on the belly.” :; °*UL’ANY BUKUR-LA’DIY ol ani bogiirladi “He ‘kid-
neyed’ him—meaning he struck him on the kidney.”
594 5 Originally YAR, altered to YAI-R- by later hand.
® MS. akramani, read akrimni.
